## 1. [Star formation history of Draco revealed by DESI DR1: suppressed early star formation efficiency for Milky Way dwarf galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26375) <a id="link1"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26375

**Authors:** Yujiao Yang, Maosheng Xiang, Yang Huang, Meng Zhang, Lanya Mou, Chao Liu, Jifeng Liu

**Abstract:** Dwarf galaxies serve as fossils for understanding the early star formation history of the local Universe. In this work, we use stellar abundances for individual stars derived from the first data release of the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI DR1) to investigate the star formation history of the dwarf spheroidal galaxy Draco. We simultaneously fit the stellar metallicity distribution function (MDF) and the [Mg/Fe]-[Fe/H] trend using the galactic chemical evolution (GCE) model flexCE. Our best-fit models imply a low star formation efficiency (SFE) of $0.003$ to $0.007\,\mathrm{Gyr}^{-1}$, which is about ten times lower than values reported in the literature, but consistent with recent SFE estimates for ultra-faint dwarf galaxies. By combining recent literature measurements, we find that the SFEs of Milky Way dwarf galaxies scale with stellar mass as $\mathrm{SFE}\propto M_\star^{0.24}$. A low star formation efficiency is associated with a strong outflow efficiency; we estimate a large wind mass-loading factor $η_{\mathrm{wind}}$ of approximately 172-260. This is well in line with the observed outflow-stellar mass relation and with the Feedback in Realistic Environment (FIRE) cosmological simulations. Our results demonstrate the power of large-scale, low-to-medium resolution stellar spectroscopic surveys to deliver robust constraints on the early formation history of galaxies in the local Universe.

**Comment:** Resolved star formation history of Draco dwarf; mass-loading factor

**Relevance:** 10
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 2. [The DARWIN Project: Cosmological Simulations of Galaxy Formation down to Dwarf Scales in Diverse Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25786) <a id="link2"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25786

**Authors:** Jaehyun Lee, Kyungwon Chun, Taysun Kimm, Yonghwi Kim, Jihye Shin, Ena Choi, Sungwook E. Hong, Myoungwon Jeon, Hannah Jhee, Minyong Jung, Hyeonyong Kim, Ji-hoon Kim, Juhan Kim, Oh-Kyoung Kwon, Hyunmi Song, Yohan Dubois, Ji-Hoon Ha, Daniel Han, San Han, Jun Yong Park, Christophe Pichon

**Abstract:** We present the DARWIN project, a suite of cosmological simulations with RAMSES to study dwarf-galaxy formation and evolution across diverse environments. DARWIN-1 is a full-box simulation in a cubic volume of $(65.5\,{\rm cMpc})^3$ with a minimum cell size of 500pc, while DARWIN-2 comprises zoom-in simulations of 24 field regions and a Milky Way analog connected to a filament, reaching 62.5pc resolution. The simulations employ baryonic physics tailored to their aims. For DARWIN-2, we include radiative transfer in three photon energy bands to trace hydrogen and helium ionization. DARWIN-1 reproduces the cosmic star formation rate density and galaxy stellar mass functions reasonably well at $z>1$. Both simulations broadly reproduce observed stellar mass-halo mass relations, chemical abundances, star-formation surface densities, and black hole-galaxy scaling relations. At lower redshift, DARWIN-1 shows an excess in the number density of massive galaxies, although the inferred stellar content of massive halos depends on the definition of galaxy stellar mass. Other systematic differences arise from spatial resolution, feedback efficiency, and environmental selection. To facilitate comparison with observations, we generate mock images using the radiative transfer codes SKIRT and Powderday. We also explore a machine-learning approach to mitigate resolution-driven biases in the dwarf galaxy properties of DARWIN-1 using the higher-resolution DARWIN-2 simulations. The DARWIN simulation suite therefore provides a comprehensive numerical framework for studying the formation, evolution, and internal physics of dwarf galaxies in a cosmological context.

**Comment:** High-resolution cosmological simulations of dwarf galaxy formation

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 3. [Searching for Dense Perturbers in Cold Dark Matter Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27236) <a id="link3"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27236

**Authors:** Xingyu Zhang, Hai-Bo Yu, Demao Kong, Ethan O. Nadler

**Abstract:** Recently, several low-mass, dense perturbers have been inferred from astrophysical observations of gravitational lensing, stellar streams, and satellite galaxies. If interpreted as dark matter subhalos, their inner densities exceed the predictions of the standard cold dark matter (CDM) model. We systematically investigate this tension using a CDM-only zoom-in simulation of a Milky Way analog. We analyze more than 5000 subhalos with masses down to $10^6\,M_\odot$ and reconstruct their inner density profiles using a tidal truncation model. The simulated subhalos are then compared with the observed compact perturbers in terms of their density profiles, enclosed masses, concentrations, and projected cylindrical masses. None of the simulated CDM subhalos is sufficiently dense to reproduce the inferred mass distributions of the observed perturbers. We further examine the redshift evolution of subhalo concentrations and derive a fitting function to characterize this evolution. Our findings motivate further investigations into exceptionally rare formation histories that could produce such dense perturbers, as well as the possibility of physics beyond the standard CDM model.

**Comment:** CDM subhalo densities vs dense perturbers, near-field cosmology

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 4. [Forecasting the Resolvability of Stellar Velocity Dispersions in Ultra-faint Dwarf Galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24563) <a id="link4"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24563

**Authors:** Pierre Thibodeaux, Alex Drlica-Wagner, Alexander P. Ji, Chin Yi Tan, Ting S. Li, Andrew B. Pace, Louis E. Strigari

**Abstract:** Ultra-faint dwarf galaxies (UFDs)---the smallest and most dark-matter dominated galactic systems---push the boundaries of our understanding of galaxy formation in the cold dark matter paradigm. As current and near-future imaging surveys continue to expand the known population of extremely faint systems, spectroscopic resources have become a major limitation in our ability to understand the physical nature of these systems. We present projections for the capability of Keck/DEIMOS, Magellan/IMACS, and future spectroscopic instruments (e.g., Magellan/Via and GMT/GMACS) to kinematically distinguish dark-matter-dominated UFDs from dark-matter-free star clusters in the ultra-faint regime. We simulate ensembles of mock observations of ultra-faint dwarf galaxies and apply a formal statistical framework, calibrated to observations, to systematically characterize the ability to resolve velocity dispersions as a function of satellite properties. Our projections suggest that current instruments will struggle to resolve many of systems that will be discovered by the Rubin Observatory. Near future instruments will be capable of resolving a majority of these systems, though they will fail for the faintest and most distant systems. Ultimately, we will require even more advanced instruments, with enhanced stability and velocity precision, to classify the full population of ultra-faint Milky Way satellites.

**Comment:** Ultra-faint dwarf satellite kinematics and dark matter content

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 5. [The Milky Way's Early Disk Was Built in Bursts. I. Evidence from Low-angular-momentum, High-$α$ Stars Scattered Out of the Protodisk by Merger Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26374) <a id="link5"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26374

**Authors:** Tiancheng Sun, Maosheng Xiang, Shaolan Bi, Jifeng Liu, Meng Zhang, Yaqian Wu

**Abstract:** Stars born in the high-$α$ disk can later acquire low angular momentum through dynamical scattering during early merger events. Their age--metallicity distribution thus provides a fossil record of the history of both star formation and dynamical perturbations in the early Milky Way disk. Here we identify a sample of 2,078 such stars from more than two million Gaia DR3 subgiants with robust determinations of ages, kinematics, and abundances. From this sample we reconstruct their intrinsic age--metallicity distribution with a forward modeling approach. Our results confirm that these scattered stars are older and more metal-poor than the bulk of the high-$α$ disk on average. In particular, the joint age--metallicity distribution reveals a robust two-component feature: a broad, dominant component with a peak age of $\sim$11.5~Gyr and ${\rm [Fe/H]}\simeq-0.59$, and an older and more metal-poor component with a peak age of $\sim$12.5~Gyr and ${\rm [Fe/H]}\simeq-0.86$. The presence of this two-component structure suggests that the high-$α$ disk experienced episodic phases of star formation and chemical enrichment. The age distribution of the younger Splash component, compared with that of the high-$α$ disk, provides evidence for a substantial dynamical perturbation at a lookback time of 10.8--11.5~Gyr, possibly associated with an early passage of Gaia--Sausage--Enceladus (GSE). The older Splash component, with a peak age of $\sim$12.5~Gyr, may preserve signatures of an earlier interaction, possibly associated with the proposed Low-energy--Kraken--Heracles (LKH) event.

**Comment:** Milky Way accreted stellar halo and merger history

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 6. [The Star Formation Histories of Different Components of M31's Halo from Photometric Estimates of Fe and $α$ Abundances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24915) <a id="link6"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24915

**Authors:** Jihye Hong, Evan Kirby, Roman Gerasimov, Masashi Chiba, Yutaka Komiyama, Itsuki Ogami, Tiffany Tang, Timothy Beers, Young Sun Lee

**Abstract:** We present the chemical-abundance distributions and star-formation histories of the M31 halo components using photometric Fe and $α$ abundance estimates derived from the PAndAS broad-band $g$ and $i$ filters and the Subaru/HSC $NB515$ narrow-band filter. We selected about 76,400 candidate M31 giant stars and constructed a synthetic photometric training dataset based on model isochrones. We estimated elemental abundances and errors using random forest regression models. We identify various halo components, including about 32,500 stars in the ``smooth" halo (where ``smooth" refers to the complete sample with all the known substructures removed), and infer their star-formation histories from comparisons of their abundance distributions. From the radial abundance gradients, we find that [Fe/H] decreases while [$α$/Fe] increases toward the outer halo. Including substructures further enhances the radial abundance contrast, suggesting that the smooth halo stars are accreted at relatively early times, before the progenitor galaxies produce large numbers of Type Ia supernovae. Both M31 and the Milky Way show similar radial gradient trends, but M31 exhibits higher [Fe/H] and lower [$α$/Fe] values overall, suggesting that the smooth halo of M31 may possess a larger fraction of relatively younger stars associated with more recent merger events. Future photometric observations with additional narrow-band filters and larger spectroscopic samples are expected to provide a better understanding of the formation and evolution history of M31.

**Comment:** Resolved star formation histories of M31 halo components

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 7. [$\texttt{GASTRO-BH}$: Effects of a Central Black Hole in GSE-like Dwarf-galaxy Mergers Around Milky Way-like Hosts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22860) <a id="link7"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.22860

**Authors:** Pedro Naethe Motta, João A. S. Amarante, Guilherme Limberg, Lais Borbolato, Chervin F. P. Laporte, Zhao-Yu Li, Reinaldo Santos-Lima, Victor P. Debattista, Marcelo C. Vicentin

**Abstract:** Recent evidence points to an intermediate-mass black hole (IMBH) in the most massive globular cluster of the Milky Way, $ω$ Centauri. This globular cluster is commonly associated with the Gaia-Sausage/Enceladus (GSE) dwarf galaxy, the last major merger event of our Galaxy, as its nuclear star cluster remnant. Motivated by this, we investigate whether the interaction between a bright GSE-like dwarf galaxy with stellar mass in the range $10^{8.5}$--$10^{9.7}$ solar masses and a Milky Way-like host can either suppress or enhance the growth of a central black hole (BH) during the merger process. To this end, we perform high-resolution $N$-body and smoothed particle hydrodynamics (SPH) simulations with the ChaNGa framework and a modified, angular-momentum-aware Bondi-Hoyle accretion prescription, considering both isolated dwarf galaxies and merger simulations with different stellar feedback efficiencies. The merger simulations show that the interaction does not halt BH accretion; instead, tidal interactions and gas inflows during pericentric passages strongly enhance accretion, leading to BH mass growth by more than two orders of magnitude in both low- and high-feedback models. The enhanced accretion is accompanied by suppressed star formation in the dwarf galaxy. Our results suggest that mergers similar to the GSE event can efficiently fuel IMBH growth in satellite galaxies rather than suppress it. This may be in tension with the estimated low mass of the proposed IMBH in $ω$ Centauri, but it could provide a mechanism for triggering active galactic nucleus activity in dwarf galaxies.

**Comment:** Dwarf galaxy merger simulations, GSE, near-field cosmology

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 8. [ASTROLABE: A 3D Particle-Level Surrogate for Cosmology Simulation with Physical Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27068) <a id="link8"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27068

**Authors:** Ibrahim Elsharkawy, David Curtin, Yonatan Kahn, Neal Weiner

**Abstract:** The microphysics of dark matter (DM) is imprinted on cosmological structure across an enormous dynamic range. Nonlinear cosmological observables are most reliably modeled with N-body and hydrodynamic simulations. However, systematic N-body scans over DM theory space are prohibitively expensive. Machine learning emulators provide a possible solution, but are currently limited by either low resolution or only the ability to emulate specific observables. We introduce Astrolabe, a flow-matching-based generative emulator designed to be free of both constraints. Astrolabe acts directly on the 6N phase-space coordinates of N individual particles. With no voxel grid in the output, there is no voxel-resolution ceiling, and in principle any observable can be measured from inference. Astrolabe is computationally tractable via a field-particle hybrid transformer in which every particle cross-attends to multiscale field tokens, so compute and memory are linear in N. Astrolabe incorporates physical flow matching by taking the prior distribution for the flow to be approximate COmoving Lagrangian Acceleration (COLA)-based coordinates rather than unphysical noise, with momentum and mass conservation enforced at all points along flow time. In the Lambda-CDM cases we test, Astrolabe produces realistic fields with accurate power spectra, halo mass functions, velocity profiles, and halo density profiles. We also observe out-of-distribution generalization, with accurate emulation of dark-acoustic oscillation initial conditions. Astrolabe is also capable of being fine-tuned to simulate very large N and we demonstrate this by emulating a TNG50-3-dark simulation at a fraction of the cost. This method represents a first step toward a general and powerful cosmology simulation surrogate, which may eventually permit the inclusion of baryonic physics and a systematic exploration of richer dark sector interactions.

**Comment:** Numerical galaxy simulations, new physics, high-resolution

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 8

## 9. [Evolution of Massive Main-Sequence Stars in Binary Population Synthesis. II. Consequences for Black Hole High-Mass X-ray Binaries and Binary Black Holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27186) <a id="link9"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27186

**Authors:** Adam Brček, Ryosuke Hirai, Ilya Mandel, Jack Yutong Chen

**Abstract:** Interactions and mass loss during the main-sequence (MS) phase of massive binary stars can significantly alter their subsequent evolution and the properties of the black holes (BHs) they ultimately produce. We use the rapid binary population synthesis code \textsc{COMPAS}, incorporating a new treatment of MS evolution that self-consistently tracks the convective core under arbitrary mass-loss or mass-gain histories. We explore the properties of black hole high-mass X-ray binaries (BH-HMXBs) and binary black holes (BBHs) formed under this new framework. We find that a more detailed treatment of MS evolution is necessary to reproduce the high BH masses observed in BH-HMXBs, which are otherwise absent under prescriptions that do not take the internal structure of MS stars into account. Matching the observed rates and orbital periods favors case A mass transfer that is more non-conservative than commonly assumed. We further find that mass transfer of helium-enriched material during case A mass transfer is common in massive binaries, with potential consequences for the structure of the accreting star. For the BBH population, the new MS framework alters the chirp-mass and mass-ratio distributions of detectable mergers, and case A mass transfer can make a substantial contribution to the BBHs formed through the stable mass transfer channel. These systems show a strong preference for near-equal mass ratios, broadly consistent with recent constraints from gravitational-wave observations.

**Comment:** New stellar evolutionary models in binary population synthesis

**Novelty:** 7

## 10. [A high-Galactic-altitude scattering screen: scintillation from Globular Cluster pulsar M53A](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25895) <a id="link10"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25895

**Authors:** Qiuyi He, Xun Shi, Dejiang Yin, Di Li

**Abstract:** While known interstellar scattering screens are largely confined to the Galactic disk, the scattering properties of the circumgalactic medium (CGM) in the deep Galactic halo have remained unexplored. Using observations from the Five-hundred-meter Aperture Spherical radio Telescope (FAST), we report the first detection of scintillation arcs in the secondary spectra of the high-latitude pulsar J1312+1810A (M53A). Under the 1D anisotropic and 2D isotropic thin-screen models considered here, the inferred scintillation screen distances are $12.8_{-3.5}^{+2.6}$ and $11.7_{-3.5}^{+2.8}$\,kpc, respectively. These model-dependent estimates correspond to Galactic heights of $12.6_{-3.5}^{+2.6}$ and $11.5_{-3.5}^{+2.7}$\,kpc, placing the candidate screen among the highest-altitude scattering structures inferred to date. The strong, time-variable scintillation asymmetry suggests substantial electron-density inhomogeneity within the screen. We speculate that this screen is associated with small-scale structures near the shock boundary of the northern eROSITA bubble. These results support the presence of discrete, inhomogeneous scattering structures deep within the Galactic halo, far beyond the regions where localized stellar feedback is typically concentrated, and highlight pulsar scintillation as a promising probe of small-scale ionized structures in the CGM.

**Comment:** CGM scattering structures probed by pulsar scintillation

## 11. [How does a galaxy's quenching probability depend on its black hole's mass?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27061) <a id="link11"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27061

**Authors:** Antonio J. Porras-Valverde, John C. Forbes

**Abstract:** We develop a parametric toy model to explore how black hole mass scatter $σ_{\rm BH}$ and the dependence of the quenching probability per unit time on black hole mass, parameterized as a power law with index $q$, jointly control the faint-end slope $α$ of the stellar mass function of quenched central galaxies. By varying $σ_{\rm BH}$ and $q$, we identify two regimes. At low scatter, $σ_{\rm BH} \lesssim 0.4$ dex, $α$ is strongly sensitive to $q$. Gradual quenching, represented by low $q$, produces shallow slopes, while sharp thresholds, with high $q$, produce steep mass functions, making $α$ a powerful diagnostic of AGN feedback prescriptions. At large scatter, $σ_{\rm BH} \gtrsim 0.6$ dex, models with vastly different $q$ converge to similar quenched mass functions, because the intrinsic scatter erases the imprint of the feedback implementation. Comparing to cosmological hydrodynamical simulations and semianalytic models, only models with $σ_{\rm BH} \gtrsim 0.4$ dex match the observed faint-end slope of $α\approx 0.7$. {\sc SAGE}, {\sc Dark Sage}, and {\sc COLIBRE}, with $σ_{\rm BH}$ of 2.0, 0.6, and 0.4 dex, respectively, reproduce this slope, whereas {\sc IllustrisTNG} and {\sc EAGLE}, with $σ_{\rm BH} \lesssim 0.2$ dex, produce steeper slopes. Current observational estimates of $σ_{\rm BH}$ allow a wide range of $q \gtrsim 0.8$, so the observed slope alone cannot identify the quenching prescription. If $σ_{\rm BH}$ is small, simultaneous measurements of $α$ and $σ_{\rm BH}$ can distinguish between competing AGN feedback models.

**Comment:** Quenching probability vs black hole mass, quenched mass function

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 12. [Reading Between the Lines: How Much Information Remains After Fitting a Stellar Spectrum?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25583) <a id="link12"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25583

**Authors:** Sven Buder, Tomasz Różánski

**Abstract:** What if we could model 100% of a stellar spectrum? Modern surveys infer stellar properties by comparing observed spectra with synthetic models, yet even the best fits leave structured residuals. We analyse 7087 GALAH survey spectra and ask what information remains after subtracting each best-fitting model. Across 13927 pixels, 4.5% of residuals exceed $3σ$, far above 0.3% expected from Gaussian noise. We map the amplitude, significance, sign, and recurrence of these residuals and identify 380 significant regions. Comparison with stellar atlases, line lists, diffuse interstellar bands, and sky diagnostics links most regions to plausible causes: 62% are candidate line-strength or line-profile mismatches, while others trace missing or unidentified lines, interstellar absorption, sky emission, or broader reduction structure. We then train data-driven models on the residual spectra themselves. Residual-only models recover $T_\mathrm{eff}$ to 80-150 K and [Fe/H] to 0.059-0.086 dex, depending on sample quality, and retain weaker, partly indirect information about elemental abundances. Extinction-related coefficients recover the strongest known interstellar features, whereas chromospheric activity leaves plausible Balmer-line signatures but is not recovered robustly out of sample. Most strikingly, the residual flux in 6% of wavelength pixels that were synthesised but excluded from the GALAH DR4 optimisation recovers stellar labels almost as well as the full residual spectrum and better than the remaining 94% alone. Residuals are therefore not merely imperfections to discard: they map missing line data, incomplete physics, unfitted astrophysical components, and observational systematics. Reading between the lines turns one spectroscopist's trash into another one's treasure, and provides a practical route towards extracting more of the information already present in stellar spectra.

**Comment:** Stellar atmosphere model residuals; relevant to stellar models

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 13. [Inferring the delay time distribution of type Ia supernovae with CAALAM I: SED modelling choices and their effect on the delay time distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23340) <a id="link13"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23340

**Authors:** Aman N. Raju, Jens Hjorth, Radosław Wojtak, Christa Gall

**Abstract:** A delay time distribution (DTD) quantifies the lag between progenitor formation and the subsequent explosive event. The DTD for a sample of Type Ia supernovae (SNe Ia) from a survey can be modelled through relative rates and the star formation histories (SFH) of all individual galaxies. Modern wide-field surveys require computationally efficient methods to model the DTD without modelling large samples of non-host galaxies. We model the DTD for a volume limited sample from SNe Ia from the Zwicky Transient Facility. The aim is to study the effect of varying SED modelling assumptions, including stellar templates, dust attenuation, SFH models, and photometric coverage. We present a new modelling framework, the Conditional Astral Ancestry Latency Analysis Machine (CAALAM), to infer the DTD of a sample of SNe Ia from the posterior of their host galaxy SFH. A model consisting of a standard stellar template, a non-parametric SFH and a flexible dust attenuation law, is favoured by the data and predicts a non-parametric DTD peaking at 100-330 Myr. When modelled as a power law, the DTD has a characteristic exponent of $-1.30 \pm 0.04$. The exponent is sensitive to dust attenuation, photometry, or SFH. When the DTD is convolved with the SFHs of the host galaxies, we find a bimodal progenitor age distribution, with $\sim60\%$ of progenitors with ages around 1.1-3.6 Gyr and the remaining progenitors older than 3.6 Gyr. We show a correspondence between the modes of the distributions of SN Ia light curve stretch and the progenitor ages. Our inferred DTD implies that most SNe Ia ($\sim 60\%$) occur promptly within 330 Myr of progenitor formation. However, the implied progenitor ages from the DTD convolved with the SFHs from our sample are significantly older. This suggests that the DTD might be a mixture of a prompt and an extended progenitor channel.

**Comment:** Host galaxy star formation histories used to infer SN Ia delay times

## 14. [Analytic and numerical study of dark matter halos in the Horndeski theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22819) <a id="link14"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.22819

**Authors:** Kohei Onoda, Shun Arai, Daisuke Yamauchi, Shuichiro Yokoyama, Shogo Masaki, Kenji Hasegawa

**Abstract:** The models of modified gravity that can explain the late-time accelerated expansion of the Universe must pass the local tests of gravity, introducing a screening mechanism so that any additional fifth force in gravity is suppressed at stellar scales. Dark matter halos, in contrast, by their extendedness in scale, may exhibit a transition of screening to small scales, providing a probe of modified gravity and the origin of the late-time acceleration. We perform comprehensive studies of dark matter halos with Vainshtein screening in the Horndeski theory. Given that dark matter is cold and a dark matter halo is spherically symmetric, we analytically clarify the conditions for the halo profile under which the Vainshtein screening works effectively. We find that the Vainshtein screening is less effective even within the Vainshtein radius as the profile approaches a core-like structure around the center of a halo, which motivates us to postulate that some features owing to the fifth force can survive during the dynamical evolution of a halo. We numerically examine our postulate by a $N$-body simulation for an isolated halo with the assumption of Newtonian two-body interactions with the spherical external fifth force. We make use of three proxies of the mass through hydrostatic dynamics, the Jeans equation, and gravitational lensing, all of which must be identical in the case of Newtonian gravity as a weak-field approximation to General Relativity. We find that these proxies can be biased relative to each other at a (quasi) equilibrium, quantitatively indicating that the fiducial model of gravity is not Newtonian. Our result affirmatively suggests that multiple mass proxies from observations are capable of testing gravity on halo scales.

**Comment:** Dark matter halo profiles and core-cusp in modified gravity

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 15. [Mass-loss Rates of 74 Bow Shock OB Stars: No Evidence for Bi-stability Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24276) <a id="link15"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24276

**Authors:** Nikhil Patten, Henry A. Kobulnicky

**Abstract:** Accurate measurements of wind-driven mass-loss rates are essential for understanding the evolution and fate of massive stars. Established techniques for measuring $\dot{M}$ suffer from large dispersions and become insensitive in the regime of weak-winded late-O to early B stars. We employ a promising new technique to measure stellar mass loss for \numstars~early type stars that produce infrared stellar bow shock nebulae. We use updated stellar parameters, improved parallactic distances and proper motions, and modern dust models to characterize the bow shock nebulae and obtain new measurements of mass-loss rates. $\mdot$ ranges from $2\times10^{-9}~\moperyear$ for weak-winded late-O and early B stars at $\log\left(\lstar/L_\odot\right)\approx4.5$ to $4\times10^{-6}~\moperyear$ for the most luminous stars in our sample at $\log\left(\lstar/L_\odot\right)\approx6.3$. Stellar space velocities relative to their local standard of rest are insufficient to accurately reflect the relative star-ISM velocities for the majority of our sample, which have speeds much less than canonical runaway thresholds, suggesting the pervasive influence of bulk interstellar flows in forming bow shock nebulae. Adopting a minimum relative velocity of $20~\kms$ is physically motivated and yields mass-loss rates in excellent agreement with the latest theoretical prescriptions while requiring no adjustable parameters. We find no enhancements in $\mdot$ for stars cooler than 25~kK, in agreement with modern mass-loss recipes that do not include bi-stability effects.

**Comment:** Stellar mass-loss models for massive stars

## 16. [Extended J-band spectroscopy of red supergiants: parameter identifiability and metallicity robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23142) <a id="link16"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23142

**Authors:** M. A. Urbaneja, R. P. Kudritzki

**Abstract:** Red supergiants (RSGs) provide a near-infrared metallicity diagnostic complementary to optical hot-supergiant spectroscopy, but their low-resolution extragalactic use requires tests of parameter identifiability and modelling robustness. We quantify the information content of extended J-band RSG spectra at R=2000 over 1.155-1.340 um and assess recovery of effective temperature, surface gravity, microturbulence, and metallicity. Using MARCS/TURBOSPECTRUM spectra and an emulator, we perform a six-node Fisher analysis and controlled mock experiments, and analyse 21 SpeX spectra of 20 RSG candidates towards Per OB1. All four parameters produce measurable responses, but only 8-10% of the raw metallicity information and 4-7% of the gravity information are independent of the other parameter directions. Extending beyond 1.22 um provides median fractions of 60% of the raw and 66% of the nuisance-orthogonal metallicity information. Gravity dominates the weakest Fisher mode, while its prior choice shifts association means by at most 0.03 dex. Matched mocks recover metallicity with an rms error of 0.04 dex. Standard continuum treatment biases solar-metallicity mocks by about -0.23 dex, reduced to about -0.01 dex by our adopted treatment. Plausible CNO mismatch adds a zero-point sensitivity of 0.04-0.05 dex, and regularised covariance changes association means by at most 0.03 dex. The preferred analysis gives a mean [Z] = -0.10 dex with a star-to-star dispersion of 0.09 dex. Extended low-resolution J-band spectra therefore yield recoverable metallicity and temperature constraints once continuum placement and weak parameter directions are controlled, although the absolute scale remains conditional on the adopted atmosphere and line-formation framework.

## 17. [Gravity-mode main-sequence asteroseismology of 16 open clusters and associations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25944) <a id="link17"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25944

**Authors:** Gang Li, Haotian Wang, Anuj Gautam, J. S. G. Mombarg, Simon J. Murphy, Mara Bernizzoni, Mingjie Jian, Saakshi Wadhwa, Dario J. Fritzewski, Facundo D. Moyano, Prasad Mani, Chenyuan Li, Timothy R. Bedding, Conny Aerts

**Abstract:** Coeval stellar populations offer a unique advantage for studying stellar physics because their members share a common age and broadly similar initial chemical composition, allowing the free parameters in stellar models to be constrained more efficiently. Meanwhile, asteroseismology, through the study of pulsation modes that propagate through stellar interiors, provides powerful constraints on internal structure and the physical processes operating beneath stellar atmospheres. The combination of coeval-population studies and asteroseismology therefore provides an unparalleled laboratory for testing and improving stellar evolutionary and structural models. Using light curves from the Kepler and TESS space missions, together with membership constraints based on Gaia astrometry, we identify g-mode period-spacing patterns in dozens of early-type ($T_{\rm eff} \gtrsim 7000\,\mathrm{K}$) main-sequence stars across 16 stellar groups. From these patterns, we infer near-core rotation rates $f_\mathrm{rot}$ and measure the asymptotic spacing $Π_0$ of g modes, tracing both angular-momentum transport and the evolution of the convective stellar cores. Using our homogeneous set of g-mode stellar-group members spanning a wide range of ages, we show that near-core rotation in early-type stars decreases with stellar evolution. We compare our results with existing model predictions. Our $Π_0$ measurements still show discrepancies with current stellar models for reasons that remain unclear, as reported in several previous studies. We also report two g-mode pre-main-sequence pulsators and three pulsating blue stragglers. Our sample provides a useful basis for calibrating stellar models in future ensemble asteroseismic studies of coeval stellar populations.

**Comment:** Asteroseismology constraining stellar evolutionary and structural models

**Novelty:** 6

## 18. [Probing the Assembly of Triangulum: Precision Metallicities and $α$ Abundances of M33's Star Clusters from APOGEE Integrated-Light Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26565) <a id="link18"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26565

**Authors:** Benjamin J. Gibson, Borja Anguiano, Sten Hasselquist, Gail Zasowski, Anil Seth, Rachael L. Beaton, Christian R. Hayes, Geraint F. Lewis, Steven R. Majewski, Giulia Pagnini

**Abstract:** The star cluster system of M33 provides an excellent opportunity to investigate whether galaxy formation physics is dependent on a galaxy's mass. Older globular clusters (GCs) trace hierarchical assembly and chemical enrichment, while younger clusters trace the recent star formation. By studying the M33 star cluster system as a whole, we can probe the formation physics of galaxies at a lower mass than the Milky Way, and look for signals of accretion. To that end, we present analysis of 37 M33 star clusters using high-resolution ($R\sim22,500$), near-infrared (1.5-1.7 $μ$m), integrated light spectra from the APOGEE survey. We use full-spectrum fitting to obtain precision measurements of the clusters' kinematics, metallicities, and $α$ abundances. We identify three populations of clusters from our measurements: young stellar clusters (~100 Myr) with broad Brackett absorption features; intermediate-age clusters (1-2 Gyr) that are metal-rich ([M/H]=-0.5 to +0.3) with roughly solar $α$ abundance; and old (>5 Gyr), kinematically-hot and metal-poor GCs. These old GCs exhibit a broad range of kinematics (RV~-250 to -8 km s$^{-1}$), metallicities ([M/H]=-1.8 to -0.6) and $α$ abundances ([$α$/M]=+0.05 to +0.3), and we identify and discuss three GCs that show strong chemodynamical evidence of accretion into M33's halo. M33's GCs at [M/H]~-1.2 have a spread in [$α$/M] of nearly 0.2 dex. This pattern is strikingly similar to the abundance distribution of Milky Way GCs, and indicates either that M33 and the Milky Way formed clusters similarly at early times, or that M33 hosts a significant population of accreted clusters in its halo.

**Comment:** Near-field cosmology: accretion signatures in M33 halo clusters

## 19. [Ionized Gas Kinematics and Star-forming Clumps in Extremely Metal-poor Galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27201) <a id="link19"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27201

**Authors:** Jyoti Yadav, Casiana Muñoz Tuñón, Cecilia Farina, Alejandro Lumbreras-Calle, Jorge Sánchez Almeida, Bruce G. Elmegreen

**Abstract:** Extremely Metal Poor (XMP) galaxies are the least chemically evolved objects in the local Universe and provide important constraints on galaxy formation in low-metallicity environments. This work investigates the ionised gas kinematics and star formation properties of XMP galaxies to identify disturbed or multi-component gas motions and explore their connection with star formation. We analyse long-slit spectroscopic observations of nine XMP galaxies obtained with the Intermediate Dispersion Spectrograph on the 2.5m Isaac Newton Telescope. The study uses the H$α$ emission line to trace ionised gas and emission-line ratios to derive gas-phase metallicities. The kinematic analysis reveals that some galaxies exhibit ordered rotation, with kinematic centres not always coincident with the luminous star-forming heads. Most systems show disturbed, asymmetric, or declining velocity profiles, while localized distortions near bright star-forming heads suggest that massive clumps influence the surrounding gas dynamics. We derive dynamical masses for the dominant clumps and investigate the mass--metallicity and size--metallicity relations. The clumps exhibit low metallicities consistent with the XMP regime and comparable to those observed in low-mass dwarf galaxies. Three galaxies show counter-rotating or kinematically decoupled ionised-gas components, indicating complex gas kinematics and possible external gas acquisition. Our sample is predominantly composed of isolated systems, with metal-poor star formation concentrated in compact head regions. These properties may reflect gas accretion through mergers, interactions, or the acquisition of metal-poor gas from the intergalactic medium and cosmic filaments. Internal processes such as outflows, gas mixing, recycling, and stochastic star formation may also contribute to the observed properties.

**Comment:** Metal-poor dwarf galaxy kinematics, outflows, gas accretion

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 5

## 20. [Inferring the shapes of low-mass galaxies at $z>6$ from FIREbox$^{HR}$. Where are the disks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26969) <a id="link20"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26969

**Authors:** Haojie Geng, Robert Feldmann, Jorge Moreno, Xuejian Shen, James Bullock

**Abstract:** Context. The three-dimensional shapes of galaxies provide clues to their formation and evolution. Inferring intrinsic shapes from projected sizes and axis ratios requires assumptions about the population distribution of shape parameters. Aims. We test commonly adopted assumptions in Bayesian shape inference for high-redshift galaxies. Methods. We analyze FIREboxHR galaxies with $\log(M_\star/M_\odot)=6.5-9.5$ at $z\sim6-15$. We measure intrinsic shapes from stellar particles using the minimum covariance determinant (MCD) estimator. We also create mock JWST/NIRCam images, measure half-light radii and axis ratios, and infer intrinsic distributions from the joint distribution of projected axis ratio $q$ and size $\log a$. Results. Low-mass galaxies are predominantly prolate, with a substantial spheroidal component. Intrinsic ellipticity $E$ and triaxiality $T$ are well described by Johnson SB distributions, while intrinsic size $A$ is approximately log-normal. We therefore model $(\mathrm{logit}(E),\mathrm{logit}(T),\log A)$ with a multivariate normal distribution and allow general correlations. This model recovers the particle-measured shape distributions more faithfully than a multivariate normal model in $(E,T,\log A)$. Triaxiality-related parameters remain difficult to constrain from projected data without large samples or informative priors. Conclusions. Bayesian forward modeling can recover intrinsic shape distributions when its population model adequately describes the underlying galaxies. An unsuitable distribution can bias inferences about morphological evolution at high redshift.

**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 21. [Metal-poor molecular clouds in dwarf galaxies at parsec-scale resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27064) <a id="link21"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27064

**Authors:** L. K. Hunt, M. Heyer, U. Lisenfeld, A. Weiss, A. Bolatto, E. Brinks, M. Rubio, D. Calzetti, F. Combes, E. Corbelli, B. G. Elmegreen, S. Garcia-Burillo, C. Henkel, K. M. Menten, L. J. Smith, A. Adamo, B. T. Draine, D. A. Hunter, N. Izumi, K. E. Johnson

**Abstract:** Understanding how stars form at low metallicity is challenging because carbon monoxide (CO) emission, which usually probes the molecular gas reservoir, is faint, and tends to be concentrated in parsec-scale clumps. To overcome this difficulty, in order to study the molecular clouds in metal-poor environments, we have acquired CO(2-1) ALMA maps at 3-4 pc resolution of four nearby dwarf galaxies with nebular (O/H) metallicities ranging from roughly 1/9 Z_sun to 1/3 Z_sun. Significant CO signal in the cubes has been identified through two masks, a ``strict'', high-confidence, mask and a ``broad'' one for better completeness. The former traces the cores of the clouds, while the latter includes fainter emission in the vicinity of the cores. Through a cloud-finding algorithm applied to the two sets of masked cubes, we have identified more than 200 molecular clouds across the sample, with roughly 50 in NGC3109, at 1/9 Z_sun, the lowest metallicity parsec-scale cloud population yet observed. The best-fit size-linewidth relations for the resolved clouds are similar among all galaxies, with a slope like the Milky Way (MW), but with lower velocity dispersion at a given cloud size. For a fixed CO-to-H_2 MW conversion factor, the cloud virial ratios tend to be large, and show a weak increasing trend with increasing cloud size, and decreasing apparent surface densities and metallicity. We find evidence for faint but significant CO emission outside CO-bright cores, spanning similar velocities to those observed in the cores. We attribute this to effective shielding by gas and dust, despite the low metallicities of our sample, since the CO tends to be cospatial with dust as traced by far-infrared emission.

## 22. [Dust in Galaxies at $z=10-15$: Attenuation and Re-emission Predictions from the FirstLight Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22766) <a id="link22"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.22766

**Authors:** Yurina Nakazato, Akio K. Inoue, Rachel S. Somerville, Daisuke Toyouchi, Kosei Matsumoto, Daniel Ceverino

**Abstract:** Dust masses and grain properties in $z>10$ galaxies remain poorly constrained, as JWST UV attenuation and ALMA dust re-emission are rarely modelled consistently. We perform post-processed 3D dust radiative transfer on 208 snapshots of 99 galaxies at $z=10$-15 from the FirstLight simulations ($M_*=10^{6.5}$-$10^{9.5}\,M_\odot$), applying twelve dust models per snapshot (2496 runs): four dust-to-metal (DTM) prescriptions and three grain size distributions. Metallicity-dependent DTM models reproduce ALMA dust-mass upper limits at $z\gtrsim10$ and detections at $z\sim7$, as dust-to-stellar mass ratios rise sharply near $Z\sim0.2\,Z_\odot$, typically at $M_*\gtrsim10^{8.5}\,M_\odot$, where grain growth by metal accretion becomes dominant. A constant DTM of 0.4 instead violates more than half of the upper limits. For dust attenuation, SMC-like dust is favored by the observed $M_{\rm UV}$-$β_{\rm UV}$ relation. The observed UV slopes ($β_{\rm UV}\simeq-2.3$) favor small grains ($a\lesssimλ_{\rm UV}/2π\simeq0.025\,μ{\rm m}$) producing steep UV extinction, together with a low abundance of carbonaceous grains or PAHs responsible for the 2175 $\mathrm{\mathring{A}}$ bump, consistent with silicate-rich dust. Large-grain stellar dust ($a_0=0.1\,μ{\rm m}$) instead yields nearly grey extinction and UV slopes close to the intrinsic value ($β_{\rm UV,0}\simeq-2.6$). SMC and MW attenuation curves flatten at $A_{\rm V}\gtrsim0.3$, showing that relatively flat attenuation does not necessarily imply large grains. For dust re-emission, dust peak temperatures reach 60-80 K, exceeding the 40-50 K commonly assumed in ALMA dust-mass estimates. Predicted IR continuum flux increases with UV luminosity; at current ALMA sensitivities (10-20 $μ$Jy), detections generally require $M_{\rm UV}\lesssim-21.5$. Roman wide-field surveys may identify such rare UV-bright galaxies.

## 23. [Self-interacting dark matter from radiative bound-state-formation feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27089) <a id="link23"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27089

**Authors:** Marcos M. Flores, Kalliopi Petraki, Thomas Pignard

**Abstract:** Observations of galactic structure motivate sizable dark-matter self-interactions at low velocities, naturally realized through light force mediators. Such mediators can also make inelastic processes very efficient. Unitarity then requires their absorptive contributions to the two-particle self-energy kernel to be resummed, which in turn feeds back into both the elastic and inelastic scattering amplitudes. We study the feedback of radiative bound-state formation on elastic scattering and its implications for self-interacting dark matter. We focus on capture processes through emission of a charged scalar; such transitions are particularly efficient because the scattering and bound states are governed by different effective potentials, a feature also shared by radiative capture through emission of a non-Abelian gauge boson. We show that their feedback can generate sizable elastic scattering relevant for halo dynamics, even when the baseline elastic amplitude vanishes. Notably, the generated elastic cross section can grow toward low velocities, resembling long-range scattering. A companion paper examines the feedback from annihilation processes.

**Novelty:** 6

## 24. [Graph Neural Networks for Astrophysics and Cosmology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27051) <a id="link24"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27051

**Authors:** Christian Kragh Jespersen

**Abstract:** Physics seldom makes breakthroughs when we consider objects only in isolation. Instead we model and measure how objects sit among their neighbours, spatial, temporal, or otherwise: a galaxy in its environment, a halo in its history, a photon hit in a detector. Graphs are ideally suited for encoding exactly these relations, and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) learn the geometric functions that respect them. This review presents the use of GNNs for astrophysics and cosmology, and argues that GNNs often suit physics and physical data better than any alternative: they fit the native geometry of the domain and the signal, enable the incorporation of the symmetries and prior knowledge we already trust and have toiled to build, and stay flexible enough to capture what we do not yet understand. GNNs' physically intuitive workings also render them more interpretable than other deep-learning architectures. We show how we can begin from general principles of the geometry of deep learning and build the graph and the graph neural network from the ground up, and how other architectures, like the convolutional network and the transformer, can be seen as special cases of one unified geometric blueprint. We survey applications across cosmology, galaxy formation, dynamics, and instrumentation; and we chart the frontier. From this survey, we find that only one in six articles outside instrumentation applies GNNs to data rather than just simulations. We also distil a set of best practices, including when not to reach for a graph. Throughout, we will prioritise physical intuition and foundational principles over mathematical machinery whenever possible.

**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 25. [Consistently Reproducing Early Galaxy Observations and 21-cm Signal during the Epoch of Reionization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24064) <a id="link25"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24064

**Authors:** Michele Bianco, Sambit K. Giri, Arghyadeep Basu, Tommaso Boschi, Aurel Schneider, Enrico Garaldi, Garrelt Mellema

**Abstract:** In the standard cosmological model, galaxies forming within dark matter haloes are the primary drivers of the Epoch of Reionization (EoR). The complex physics of galaxy formation introduces scatter in the relation between halo mass and star-formation activity, with implications for the production and escape of ionising photons. In this work, we implement a stochastic source model in our cosmological radiative transfer simulations. We calibrate our model against the high-redshift ultraviolet luminosity function observed by JWST and observational constraints on Lyman-continuum leakage to constrain the ionising escape fraction. We find that stochastic star formation redistributes ionising emissivity toward lower-mass haloes, producing a more fragmented ionisation topology and delaying the growth of the largest ionised regions relative to the deterministic, observationally calibrated model. However, when the two models match total ionising photon production, their global neutral fractions and 21-cm power spectrum remain nearly indistinguishable, despite small differences in the morphology of individual ionised regions. Additionally, we establish a unified framework that lets us apply source models derived from computationally expensive hydrodynamical simulations (SPICE, FIRE-2 and Thesan) and investigate their impact on large-volume reionisation simulations, extending their prescriptions to scales of >100 Mpc. We find that these prescriptions produce distinct ionisation topologies and 21-cm statistical signatures, even when their global reionisation histories are similar. Our results highlight the importance of combining physically motivated galaxy formation prescriptions from hydrodynamical simulations with large-volume radiative transfer simulations to interpret forthcoming 21-cm observations and constrain the sources of cosmic reionisation.

**Novelty:** 6

## 26. [Connecting galaxy structure, quenching, and halo environments during cosmic noon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25446) <a id="link26"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25446

**Authors:** Ziwen Zhang, Cheng Jia, Huiyuan Wang, Enci Wang, Yangyao Chen, Houjun Mo, Xiong Luo, Cheqiu Lyu, Jie Song

**Abstract:** The connection between galaxy structure, star formation activity, and large-scale environments provides important insights into the evolution of massive galaxies. However, the relative importance of different galaxy properties in predicting quenching and the environmental dependence of structurally distinct galaxy populations remain poorly constrained at cosmic noon ($1\leq z<3$). In this work, we investigate massive galaxies with $\log M_*/{\rm M}_{\odot}>10.5$ at $1\leq z<3$ using deep JWST/NIRCam imaging from the COSMOS-Web survey, combining measurements of central stellar mass surface density ($Σ_1$), star formation activity, and galaxy clustering. We find that $Σ_1$ is the most informative predictor of quenching among the considered properties, including stellar mass, effective radius, formation redshift, and local overdensity. The quenched fraction shows a strong dependence on $Σ_1$, increasing rapidly above a characteristic $Σ_1$. We further show that compact quenched galaxies exhibit a tendency toward stronger clustering than compact and extended star-forming galaxies, while compact and extended star-forming galaxies show similar clustering properties. These results highlight the importance of $Σ_1$ in tracing quenching and reveal that structural diversity among star-forming galaxies is not strongly reflected in their large-scale environments, providing new constraints on the interplay between galaxy structure, quenching, and halo environments during cosmic noon.

## 27. [The Impact of an AGN on PAH Spectral Features in Galaxy Evolution Simulations: Continuum Dilution and Excitation of PAHs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24778) <a id="link27"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24778

**Authors:** Grant P. Donnelly, Desika Narayanan, Paul Torrey, J. -D. T. Smith, Helena M. Richie, Hui Li, Federico Marinacci

**Abstract:** Galaxies hosting an active galactic nucleus (AGN) show diminished power and non-standard spectral feature ratios from polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) compared with normal star-forming galaxies. Proposed mechanisms for this difference include a combination of AGN-driven PAH grain processing, differential heating from the surrounding radiation field, and continuum dilution. These potentially competing factors are difficult to disentangle observationally. In this paper, we investigate the impact of the AGN radiation field on the excitation properties and potential dilution of the mid-IR emission features via constrained numerical experiments of idealized galaxies in simulations that include a self-consistent model for the evolution of dust. We find that, for the integrated spectrum of our simulated Milky Way analog and independent of grain-processing physics, the apparent PAH emission is progressively diminished as AGN luminosity is increased. For spectra within the central half kiloparsec, PAH features are entirely obfuscated when an AGN of any sampled luminosity is present. We additionally find that the UV radiation from an AGN is capable of impacting PAH band ratios, though note that we do not yet include a model for potential photodestruction of PAHs. Our model suggests that the inferred lack of PAHs via featureless mid-IR spectra from galaxies hosting AGN may simply owe to the direct continuum from the AGN overpowering mid-IR features from PAHs in the ISM of the host galaxy. Future work will investigate the relative role of AGN continuum dilution and PAH heating vs photodestruction/ionization of PAHs.

## 28. [Gaia in Technicolor. I. A Simple Variability-Encoded Color-Magnitude Diagram](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22804) <a id="link28"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.22804

**Authors:** Brad N. Barlow, Bennett C. Kirby

**Abstract:** The Hertzsprung-Russell diagram is one of the most important tools in stellar astrophysics. In its modern, empirical form as the Gaia color-magnitude diagram (CMD), each star is represented with a point determined from its time-averaged brightness and color. However, many stars are intrinsically variable, and the fixed-point representation ignores the variety of ways in which a star can vary. As a system pulsates, eclipses, is irradiated, erupts, or otherwise varies, it moves through the CMD along a trajectory whose size, slope, and shape are determined by the physics driving the brightness and color variability. Here we exploit the near-simultaneous, three-band, multi-epoch photometry (MEP) released for ~11.7 million sources in Gaia DR3 to construct a variability-encoded CMD (VECMD). In this updated take on the traditional CMD, each star is represented not by a point but by a linear segment approximating the path it traces in absolute magnitude and color as it varies. Because these trajectories are built from the raw epoch data, they are agnostic to period and do not require phase-folding. We show that radial-mode pulsators trace looped, Lissajous-like trajectories that can encode the phase lag between radius and temperature. The Hertzsprung bump is even visible in the CMD loops of some Cepheids. Variable binary systems can trace tight color-correlated lines and/or hooked paths. Accreting and dust-obscured systems trace long, high-amplitude tracks aligned with the outburst or reddening vector. Distinct regions of the VECMD are dominated by distinct trajectory morphologies. We argue that these trajectories are period-independent fingerprints well suited to machine learning classification, which we will pursue on the much larger epoch-photometry sample expected in Gaia DR4. Finally, we present a free app called IVECMD for finding, classifying, and analyzing variable stars in the VECMD.

**Novelty:** 6

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26560

**Authors:** Pierluigi Rinaldi, Javier Álvarez-Márquez, Alejandro Crespo Gómez, Luis Colina, Stefano Carniani, Sarah Kendrew, Edoardo Iani, Angela Adamo, Abdurro'uf, Marianna Annunziatella, Stacey Alberts, Leindert A. Boogaard, Karina I. Caputi, Marco Castellano, Michele Catone, Rui Marques-Chaves, Ryan Cooper, Seiji Fujimoto, Mahmoud Hamed, Yuichi Harikane, Santosh Harish, Takuya Hashimoto, Jakob M. Helton, Tiger Y. -Y. Hsiao, Vasily Kokorev, Álvaro Labiano, Jens Melinder, Ikki Mitsuhashi, Göran Östlin, Lorenzo Napolitano, Borja Pérez-Díaz, Pablo G. Pérez-González, Carlota Prieto Jiménez, Giulia Rodighiero, Paola Santini, Ana Varo-O'Ferrall, Jorge A. Zavala, Yongda Zhu

**Abstract:** Detailed UV-to-optical studies of galaxies at z >= 10 have largely focused on bright systems (MUV <= -20 mag), leaving intrinsically faint galaxies virtually unexplored to date. Combining NIRCam, NIRSpec, and MIRI imaging and spectroscopy, we investigate UNCOVER-ABELL2744-13151 (U13151), an ultra-faint galaxy at z = 9.7995 (MUV = -17.44 mag), magnified by Abell 2744 ($μ$ = 12.8). U13151 is very blue ($β$ ~ -2.64), ultra-low mass (log10(M*/Msun) ~ 6.68), young (~ 5.5 Myr), metal-poor (Z ~ 3-6% Zsun), and compact (Re ~ 122 pc). It exhibits negligible dust attenuation, extreme ionization conditions, and intense star formation (sSFR(Ha) ~ 94 Gyr-1, or equivalently M*/SFR(Ha) ~ 10.6 Myr). Despite similarities with much brighter galaxies, its low C/O, approximately solar Ne/O, and undetected nitrogen emission suggest enrichment dominated by core-collapse supernovae, in stark contrast with similar nitrogen-enhanced systems. U13151 might be undergoing its first major episode of star formation, assembling most (if not all) of its M* through five unresolved clumps. Its high ionizing photon production efficiency (log10($ξ_{\rm ion}$/ (Hz erg-1)) ~ 25.64) suggests, through a simple toy model, suggests, through a simple toy model, that similarly short-lived episodes could contribute significantly to the ionizing photon budget of low-mass galaxies during Cosmic Reionization. We explore different evolutionary scenarios, from U13151-like galaxies potentially representing the progenitors of Blue Monsters at higher redshifts to the possibility that such systems could be the early building blocks of present-day Milky Way-like galaxies. Our results highlight the fundamental role of MIRI in studying galaxies in the early Universe and the urgent need to investigate the faintest, lowest-mass systems to shed light on the fundamental physics driving the earliest phases of galaxy formation.

## 30. [Evolving only the observed spacetime region: perfectly parallel lightcone simulations with sCOLA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26639) <a id="link30"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26639

**Authors:** Mayeul Aubin, Florent Leclercq, Guilhem Lavaux

**Abstract:** Cosmological analyses of Stage-IV galaxy surveys require large ensembles of lightcone mock catalogues, but a full survey-scale $N$-body run costs of order $10^7$ CPU hours, putting such ensembles out of reach. We present a framework that produces survey-scale dark matter lightcones for a typical cost of order $10^3$ CPU hours each, based on the spatial COmoving Lagrangian Acceleration (sCOLA) method. sCOLA decomposes an arbitrarily large volume into small tiles that are evolved independently, with buffer regions and non-periodic boundary conditions on the gravitational potential. We combine it with on-the-fly lightcone building, so that the evolved spacetime region reduces to the observed one: each tile is stopped as soon as the observer's past lightcone has left it, and only the tiles intersecting the region of interest are simulated at all. We further introduce two accuracy improvements to the sCOLA formalism: an LPT buffer that approximates the inflow of particles from outside the sCOLA box, and a second-order perturbative approximation to the gravitational potential at its boundaries; together they remove the large-scale inaccuracies of previous work and allow smaller tiles and buffers. Against a \texttt{Gadget4} reference in a $3.84\,h^{-1}\,\mathrm{Gpc}$ box with $3\,840^3$ particles, up to $k \simeq 0.4\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}$ the sCOLA lightcone reproduces the matter power spectrum to 1%, the velocity divergence power spectrum to 1.5% and the equilateral bispectrum to 2%, with no visible discontinuity at tile boundaries. The run cost $1\,300$ CPU hours, a factor of $2\,600$ below our $\texttt{Gadget4}$ run. Perfect parallelism at this cost brings ensembles of thousands of survey-scale lightcones within reach of a single allocation: $\sim 25\,000$ realisations of the $\textit{Euclid}$ Galactic north cap with a mass resolution of $10^{11}\,\mathrm{M}_\odot$ for $10^{7}$ CPU hours.

## 31. [How bright can a galaxy be? Extreme value predictions for high-redshift survey observables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23016) <a id="link31"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23016

**Authors:** Christopher C. Lovell, Theodore Martin, Sandro Tacchella, Christian K. Jespersen, Ian Harrison

**Abstract:** Assessing the significance of extreme galaxy candidates has typically relied on redshifts and stellar / halo masses inferred through inverse modelling, which carries substantial systematic uncertainty. We combine the halo mass function with a conditional model for galaxy emission and apply the Extreme Value Statistics (EVS) formalism to predict the distribution of the observed flux of the brightest galaxy expected in a given survey area, redshift interval and photometric band. We find the choice of parametric star formation history (SFH) dominates the modelling uncertainty, introducing a spread approaching $\sim 1 \, \mathrm{dex}$ by $z \sim 18$, whilst the stellar population synthesis model, initial mass function and metallicity each contribute $\lesssim 0.3 \, \mathrm{dex}$. The luminous $z \gtrsim 9$ candidates in COSMOS-Web cluster about the median prediction for the area they were drawn from, and lie at least an order of magnitude below the $3σ$ limit, a margin comparable to the SFH systematic. Of the observational samples reaching beyond $z \sim 13$, the two spectroscopically confirmed galaxies at $z \simeq 14$ lie close to the median prediction for their area, whereas photometric candidates at $z \gtrsim 16$ reach $+4σ$ (a probability to exceed of $\sim 10^{-5}$), the brightest lying at the limit obtained by converting all halo baryons into stars. Since the prediction bounds the flux far more tightly than the photometry bounds the redshift, such offsets are most naturally resolved by errors in the inferred redshifts. This approach allows extreme candidates to be assessed directly in the observational plane, making explicit the modelling assumptions common to forward and inverse approaches.

## 32. [The Next Generation Virgo Cluster Survey (NGVS). XXIX. Demography of Nuclear Star Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25527) <a id="link32"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25527

**Authors:** Max M. Kurzner, Patrick Côté, Laura Ferrarese, Chelsea Spengler, Eric W. Peng, Rubén Sánchez-Janssen, Joel C. Roediger, Kaixiang Wang, Chengze Liu, Sungsoon Lim, Matthew A. Taylor, Eleanore B. Todd, Patrick R. Durrell, Elisa Toloba, Puragra Guhathakurta, J. Christopher Mihos, John P. Blakeslee, Hong-Xin Zhang

**Abstract:** Nuclear star clusters (NSCs) provide insights into processes shaping galaxy cores. We use deep $u^*giz$ CFHT imaging from the Next Generation Virgo Cluster Survey (NGVS) to study NSCs in Virgo cluster galaxies. Our parent sample consists of 3680 certain, likely or possible cluster members spanning more than six orders of magnitude in stellar mass. Using a self-consistent classification framework, we identify 1144 candidate nucleated galaxies: 804 high-confidence NSCs and 340 lower-confidence candidates. This is the largest and most uniform NSC catalog for any environment studied to date. We explore the nucleation fraction, $f_{\rm nuc}$, as a function of stellar mass, morphology and environment. Consistent with previous studies, we find stellar mass a key factor driving nucleation probability. However, host-galaxy morphology is also important, with the peak of the $f_{\rm nuc}$ distribution occurring at progressively lower masses as one moves from later to earlier morphological types. We interpret this shift as evidence that the more extended structures of late-type galaxies reduce the efficiency of GC inspiral at fixed stellar mass, while gas-rich secular processes and central star formation may contribute increasingly to NSC growth at higher masses. Environment plays a smaller role, with only a slight enhancement in high-density regions. We characterize the nonlinear relationship between NSC stellar mass and host-galaxy mass, and assess the role of selection effects. We conclude that NSC formation is best explained by a hybrid scenario, with inspiraling star clusters and in situ star formation contributing, and their relative importance dictated primarily by galaxy mass.

## 33. [Core-Collapse Supernova Rates in the Dark Energy Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27328) <a id="link33"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27328

**Authors:** K. Boyne, O. Graur, M. Vincenzi, P. Wiseman, M. Grayling, M. Smith, C. Frohmaier, C. Lidman

**Abstract:** Over its five-year campaign (2013-2017), the Dark Energy Survey (DES) supernova (SN) survey recorded 31,636 light curves. Using a custom-built, two-stage random forest classifier, we identified 2,445 likely core-collapse SNe (CC SNe) within the survey rate analysis footprint. With this sample, we measure the volumetric CC SN rate from redshift z = 0 to 1.3 in 11 redshift bins, representing the most detailed single-survey coverage to date across nine billion years of lookback time. Over this interval, the CC SN rates are well fit by a power law R_CC(z) = R_0 (1 + z)^α, with R_0 = 1.58 \pm 0.17 \times 10^{-4} \text{ yr}^{-1} \text{ Mpc}^{-3} and α= 2.85 \pm 0.25. The rate turnover, expected at cosmic noon, is not yet apparent. Because CC SNe trace recent star formation, we compare our measurements with recent cosmic star-formation history (CSFH) models. Our measurements are ~70 per cent higher than CSFH models using a Salpeter initial mass function (IMF) to predict CC SN rates. Replacing the Salpeter IMF with a Baldry and Glazebrook IMF and a CC SN progenitor mass range of 8-40 M_\odot bridges the gap. Alternatively, CSFHs may miss a hitherto unidentified population of low-luminosity galaxies. We further find that the local ratio of stripped-envelope (SE) SNe to all CC SNe is 31 \pm 5 per cent, remaining constant from z = 0 to 1.3. Combined with an IMF analysis accounting for the "missing progenitor problem" of hydrogen-rich SNe, our results favour a mixture of single massive stars and binaries as SE SN progenitors, with the redshift-invariant ratio favouring binary channels.

## 34. [Slowly Rotating Fully Convective Stars at $\lesssim$ 1 Gyr: Evidence from the Hyades and Praesepe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27112) <a id="link34"></a>

**Abstract:** We report the discovery of 10 stars in the Praesepe and Hyades clusters with unusually slow rotation that are likely not field contamination. Even though our search was general, all slow rotators are in the fully convective domain. We find that the fraction of slow rotators in the clusters is more than $5σ$ above the distribution expected from field contamination. They provide a potential link between the period distribution of the young open clusters and the field. These stars could be a distinct birth population, or they could be an indication that the onset of strong magnetic breaking is earlier than previously realized in this domain. Photometric variability and X-ray luminosity suggest the later but more data is needed to confirm. Searches for similar populations in younger clusters will help determine when slow rotators first emerge and constrain the timescale and physical mechanisms governing angular-momentum loss in fully convective stars. The discovery of these stars also highlights the importance of long-baseline photometric observations for detecting the long rotation periods of low-mass stars.

## 35. [A MUSE Study of Stellar and Ionized-Gas Kinematics in Central Group Galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27169) <a id="link35"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27169

**Authors:** V. Olivares, E. O'Sullivan, P. Temi, S. Suárez, K. Kolokythas, M. Gaspari, F. Ubertosi, M. Aravena, F. Combes, Y. Su, P. Salome, E. Treister, M. Gitti, F. Brighenti, M. -L. Gendron-Marsolais

**Abstract:** Understanding gas accretion and feedback in low-mass galaxy groups bridges the gap between isolated ellipticals and cluster-dominant galaxies. We present new Multi Unit Spectroscopic Explorer (MUSE) observations of 17 nearby brightest group galaxies (BGGs), revealing diverse origins for their gas reservoirs. Warm ionized gas, traced by Halpha and [N II], is detected in 77% (13/17) of the sample, with morphologies including rotating disks with or without tails and diffuse emission, rings, and filaments. Unlike brightest cluster galaxies, most BGGs host regularly rotating stars, whereas their gas displays diverse kinematics. Nearly half of the rotating gas disks are aligned with the stars, consistent with internal recycling. However, tails, diffuse emission, or rings in three aligned systems may retain signatures of past accretion or interactions. The remaining disks, ring-like structures, and most filamentary systems are misaligned, supporting gas acquisition through mergers, interactions, or halo cooling. Compact disks are consistent with centrally concentrated, low-angular-momentum gas, possibly linked to residual cooling. Most filamentary systems lie near the precipitation threshold (tcool/teddy ~ 1-2) and preferentially occupy the turbulence-dominated region of the k-plot, supporting condensation consistent with chaotic cold accretion. All galaxies with detected X-ray halos, including those without warm gas, exhibit low central entropies (<30 keV cm^2) and short central cooling times (<3 Gyr). These conditions alone cannot predict multiphase gas, suggesting that AGN- or interaction-driven perturbations and turbulence-induced thermal instabilities help regulate condensation. No single mechanism dominates: BGGs are transitional systems in which internal recycling, environmental accretion, and cooling-driven condensation coexist, linking isolated ellipticals to cluster-dominant galaxies.

**Novelty:** 5

## 36. [Beyond the Disc--Spheroid Dichotomy at $0.6 < z < 8.0$: Evidence for a Morphological Indeterminacy Regime from JWST](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24078) <a id="link36"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24078

**Authors:** Igor Kolesnikov, Reinaldo Ramos de Carvalho, Jeong Hwan Lee, Rodrigo Flores-Freitas, Vitor M. Sampaio, Luiz A. Silva-Lima

**Abstract:** Galaxy morphology is a fundamental tracer of galaxy assembly, but its characterization becomes increasingly uncertain in the early Universe. We investigate morphological classifications across different methodologies over the redshift range $0.6 < z < 8.0$, using JWST observations of 15,567 galaxies with 6,099 synthetic galaxies from IllustrisTNG. To quantify morphology without relying on visual labels or prescribed light profiles, we use an unsupervised-supervised hybrid machine-learning approach based on the non-parametric MEGG system, which combines the second moment of the light distribution, Entropy, Gini, and Gradient Pattern Analysis. We find that parametric and non-parametric classifications remain broadly consistent at $z < 2.5$, but diverge systematically at higher redshift. We identify this epoch as the Morphological Indeterminacy Regime (MIR), in which classical disc and spheroidal categories no longer provide a unique description of the galaxy population. The fraction of mixed-interpretation galaxies increases with redshift, reaching $F_{\rm MIR} \sim 0.55$ at $z > 2.5$. Cross-domain and projection-invariance tests show that the classifications are stable and insensitive to data set and viewing angle. In the interval $2.5 < z < 8.0$, MIR galaxies occupy an intermediate stellar-mass regime (median; $\log(M_\star/M_\odot) \sim 9.7$), between low-mass discs (median; $\log(M_\star/M_\odot) \sim 9.5$) and massive spheroids (median; $\log(M_\star/M_\odot) \sim 10.1$), while irregular systems have stellar-mass distributions similar to those of MIR galaxies. We interpret these results as evidence that morphological indeterminacy is a characteristic feature of galaxy assembly in the early Universe, reflecting a population of dynamically young systems that have not yet settled into the classical Hubble sequence.

## 37. [Extending the M33 Extinction Map with Radio and Far-Infrared Gas Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26571) <a id="link37"></a>

**Authors:** Yuxi Wang

**Abstract:** As the third-largest galaxy in the Local Group, M33 requires high-precision, wide-area extinction maps to recover the intrinsic luminosity and enable accurate studies, yet the available maps remain limited in coverage. In this work, we extend the stellar-based extinction map within the Panchromatic Hubble Andromeda Treasury: Triangulum Extended Region (PHATTER) footprint to the outer disk by calibrating empirical $A_V$-H\,\textsc{i} and $A_V$-total hydrogen relations in the PHATTER overlap region. Within the PHATTER footprint, we retain the stellar-based map at 6$^{\prime\prime}$ resolution, and outside the area covered by the stellar-based extinction map we produce an extended map at $\approx 12^{\prime\prime}$ resolution. The resulting map preserves a continuous morphology across the PHATTER boundary and reveals clear spiral-arm and interarm contrast and patchy dust substructures across a broader area. The median and maximum extinction values, $\mathrm{median}(A_V)\approx 0.91$ mag and $A_{V,\mathrm{max}} \approx 2.62$ mag, are consistent with the high-extinction range reported by previous M33 studies, while naturally reflecting differences in tracers and spatial coverage. The extinction map provides a practical, publicly available dataset for galaxy-wide extinction correction and for further studies in M33.

## 38. [Mode power and stability in radial post-AGB pulsations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25166) <a id="link38"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25166

**Authors:** Jan Zalewski

**Abstract:** We examine the relation between mode power and excitation rate for radial pulsations along a sequence of high-luminosity post-AGB envelope models. The net thermodynamic exchange power, including boundary contributions, is compared with the excitation rate and the inertia, compression and horizontal area deformation norms entering the balance relation. We find that positive net exchange power can accompany damping, while negative net exchange power can accompany excitation. Along an individual mode sequence, the power may reverse sign repeatedly without a change of stability. These reversals are associated with changes in the sign of the effective norm. Including the compression contribution in the pressure-force power does not remove this behaviour and can extend the intervals of sign disagreement. Thus, although spatial exchange integrals identify positive and negative contributions to the modal power, the sign of the global power alone does not generally determine mode stability.

## 39. [Comparing Infrared & X-ray-selected Active Galactic Nuclei host galaxy star formation rates to the galaxy main sequence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23349) <a id="link39"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23349

**Authors:** Dustin T. Mason, James R. Mullaney, Liam P. Grimmett

**Abstract:** A key method of exploring the relationship between active galactic nuclei (AGN) and their host galaxies is to determine the astrophysical properties of galaxies that host AGN, and compare them to those that do not. Star formation rates are believed to be strongly related to nuclear activity, both indirectly via their common fuel supply, and directly via the energy injected by the AGN either suppressing or enhancing star formation. Strong observational evidence of a direct link between nuclear activity and in-situ star formation has proven elusive, likely in part due to the very different timescales of nuclear activity and episodes of star formation. To overcome these challenges, some studies have demonstrated that evidence of the aforementioned link may be found in the distributions of AGN power and star formation rates. While previous attempts to determine these distributions have largely focused on X-ray-selected samples of AGN, in this study we also attempt to determine the star formation rate distributions of infrared-selected AGN. The results vary between our samples, but at low redshift (0.8 < z < 1.2) we find that more powerful X-ray-selected AGN are more likely to occur in galaxies with higher specific star formation rates, consistent with previous studies. At higher redshifts (1.0 < z < 1.8), this trend is reversed with higher power AGN tending to occur in galaxies with lower specific star formation rates. By contrast, infrared-selected galaxies at 1.0 < z < 1.8 show no evidence of a changing star formation rate distribution with AGN power.

**Abstract:** We present a near-infrared spectroscopic follow-up study of seven H$α$ emitter candidates at $z\sim2$ in the ZFOURGE-COSMOS field, selected via the $K_{\rm s}$-band flux excess method. Spectra obtained with Subaru/SWIMS confirm strong H$α$ emission line at $z=2.06-2.49$ for all the targets. The low-mass galaxies show consistent H$α$ flux measurements between the $K_{\rm s}$-band flux excess method and the spectroscopy, whereas the high-mass galaxies show higher H$α$ flux inferred from the $K_{\rm s}$-band flux excess than the spectroscopic measurement which may be attributed to their extended star formation distributions. They are characterized by high [O III]/H$β$ ratios ($>4$) and weak [O II] and [N II] emission lines, indicating high ionization parameters and low metallicity. The line ratios are comparable to those of star-forming galaxies at $z \gtrsim 6$ and their local analogs. JWST/NIRCam imaging reveals a clear mass-dependent color gradient: the low-mass galaxies present positive F115W-F277W radial gradients with blue cores, while the high-mass galaxies exhibit negative gradients with red cores. These results suggest distinct assembly pathways for low- and high-mass galaxies during the cosmic noon epoch.
